Like I’ll be thinking about finances, and then my mind jumps to my cousin, like, “Was she annoyed with me?” or “Did I say something weird?” just because a text sounded a little short.\n\nAnd then at work it’ll switch again. I’ll be on a support call or about to respond to someone and start thinking, “What if I make a mistake?” or “What if I explain it badly and they think I don’t know what I’m doing?” So it’s not really one main area anymore. It kind of bounces around to whatever feels like it could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are usually about things that could realistically happen, or are they often about unlikely scenarios?\n\nPatient: I think that’s what makes it confusing, because a lot of them are technically realistic. Like, it’s not impossible that my cousin could be upset with me, or that I could make a mistake on a support call, or that money could be tight and I’ll struggle to save again. Those things could happen.\n\nBut the way my mind treats them feels bigger than the actual situation, if that makes sense. A short text from my cousin turns into me wondering if I damaged the relationship somehow. One work call turns into, “What if I mess this up and everyone thinks I’m bad at my job?” And then the condo or money stuff becomes this whole spiral about how I’m ever going to move forward with my life.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say they’re always totally unrealistic scenarios. It’s more that my worry grabs onto things that are possible, and then it stretches them out until they feel much more threatening than they probably are.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control your worries most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not most of the time, no. I can sometimes interrupt them for a little bit, but it doesn’t feel like I’m really in control of them.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, I already checked the spreadsheet,” or “I already sent that email, and it looked fine,” and I’ll try to move on. And for maybe a few minutes I can focus on something else. But then the thought comes back, almost like it was waiting there. It’s not even always a new concern — it’s the same thing looping again, like, “But what if you missed something?” or “What if you sent it with the wrong attachment?” or “What if someone notices and thinks you’re careless?”\n\nAnd then once that starts, it doesn’t stay as one thought. One “what if” turns into five more before I can really pull myself back. I’ll know I’m doing it, too, which is the frustrating part. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “You already went over this,” but it still doesn’t feel settled. It’s like reassurance only works briefly, and then my mind starts arguing with it again. So I wouldn’t say I can control it most of the time. I can try to manage it, but it often feels like it keeps going on its own.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. It’s very often about what might happen next, or what something could turn into. Like, it doesn’t just stay on the condo anymore, even though that’s still there in the background. My mind will jump ahead to, “What if I can’t save money again?” or “What if I never really get back on track or move forward with my life?”\n\nAnd then it’ll jump to people, like with my cousin — if she seems a little off or sends a short reply, I start worrying that maybe she’s upset with me, and then I’m imagining what that means later, like maybe things will become awkward or I’ll have damaged the relationship somehow.\n\nOr at work, before or during a support call, I’ll start thinking ahead like, “What if I make a mistake?” or “What if I don’t explain something clearly?” It’s not always something that has happened. It’s more like my brain keeps scanning for the next thing that could go wrong, and then I’m already reacting to it as if it’s about to happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It used to feel like the condo was the main thing, or at least that was the easiest thing to point to. Like, if someone asked me what I was stressed about, I could say, “Oh, it’s the condo situation,” and that would sound concrete.

But honestly, it doesn’t really stay there anymore. It sort of spreads. One day it’s my cousin, like if she sends a short text or seems a little distant, I’ll start wondering if she’s upset with me or if I did something wrong. Then the next minute it’s work, like worrying I’ll make a mistake on a support call or not explain something clearly and then it’ll reflect badly on me. And then it jumps back to money, like how I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life.

So I don’t know if there’s one single area where it originates now. It feels more like my mind just keeps moving from one possible problem to the next, and each one feels urgent while I’m thinking about it.
